**SECTION 2: ADVISOR AND TEAM REFLECTIONS**

**Advisor.** Over the past ten weeks, it has been a rewarding experience to witness the growth of the Greenox team through their participation in the Wharton Global High School Investment Competition. The Greenox team engaged in rigorous research, portfolio analysis, and collaborative decision-making throughout their investing journey. This has enabled them to develop a deeper understanding of investing principles and the complexities of investment management. Importantly, they grew as collaborators and as a team - sharing ideas, debating strategies, and making data-driven decisions as a team. As Warren Buffett wisely said, “The best investment you can make is in yourself.”, the Greenox team have truly invested in their own learning. Watching their transformation from curious learners to informed decision-makers has been inspiring.

**Team.** Inspired by Connor Barwin’s focus on long-term community-led change, we want to apply what this competition taught us to address a gap at our own Australian school, where many peers lack practical experience managing money. Managing Connor’s portfolio showed us that investment decisions carry real consequences. Our FMC loss cost $5,400, capital that could have funded half a year of MTWB distributions, and forced us to professionalize through written memos, red-teaming, and cooling periods. We learned to think beyond quarterly returns by focusing on sequence risk. Could the portfolio withstand a 2008-level crisis without forced liquidation to fund annual distributions? This shift from theoretical optimization to practical stewardship is what we aim to share through peer-led workshops and portfolio simulations. Managing real capital for a real mission taught us that investing is not about elegant frameworks, but about building portfolios that accomplish objectives under real-world constraints.

**SECTION 3: TRADING NOTE ANALYSIS** 

**(ETF) BATS: PAVE (Global X US Infrastructure Development ETF)**[image]

**Reflection:** PAVE provided diversified exposure to the U.S. infrastructure capex cycle, translating a broad political and economic theme into a disciplined portfolio position. We used PAVE to hardwire our “Renovation” philosophy into the portfolio, gaining exposure to roads, grids, utilities, and industrial assets without relying on single-stock timing. Its 99 holdings across construction materials, heavy machinery and engineering reduced concentration risk, which was critical given infrastructure’s dependence on multi-year funding cycles. While infrastructure spending can be cyclical, PAVE’s breadth allows us to express long-term conviction in physical renewal while mitigating regulatory and execution risk inherent in individual names.

**(Stock) NYSE: JNJ (Johnson and Johnson)**

[image]

JNJ taught us that resilient infrastructure is not only physical, but human. Healthcare emerged as essential “human infrastructure,” particularly as aging populations drive sustained demand for medical services. JNJ’s low volatility profile (beta 0.52) and long dividend history made it a Foundation holding designed to stabilize the portfolio during market stress. Its 3.1% dividend yield supported MTWB’s $10,000 annual distributions, reducing the likelihood of forced equity sales during downturns. Pairing JNJ with more cyclical infrastructure names reinforced that stewardship requires balancing growth with reliability, ensuring capital remains productive even when markets are unstable.

**(Team Choice) NYSE: XYL (Xylem Inc)**

Reflection:[image]

Xylem made our strategy tangible by linking investment decisions directly to public infrastructure that affects daily life. Its focus on water systems aligned closely with MTWB’s stormwater and resilience initiatives, making it a high-conviction Construction holding. The premium valuation forced us to debate growth versus price discipline, but we concluded that Xylem’s position in a replacement-driven water infrastructure cycle justified the trade-off. Municipal water systems face decades of deferred maintenance, and Xylem operates as a critical intermediary with limited substitutes. This decision reinforced that mission alignment alone is insufficient; it must be supported by durable demand and long-term capital deployment.

**SECTION 4: PORTFOLIO-AT-A-GLANCE**

Connor Barwin’s philanthropy renovates infrastructure compounding in a way community compound trust. He assesses needs, pours foundations, constructs facilities, and adds transformative features that turn neglected lots into permanent community assets stewarding social value for decades. We apply this “renovation” philosophy to capital allocation.

Growing $500,000 to $1.5 million over 10 years while funding annual $10,000 grants demands 11.6% returns. This aggressive target conflicts with Barwin's moderate risk tolerance, but his 10-year horizon resolves the tension. We built a portfolio absorbing short-term volatility margins to capture structural growth that outperforms over a long economic cycle. Greenox treats capital as infrastructure, executing this through a three-sleeve “renovation” [1].

**(Renamed from Preserve) Foundation (30%)** preserves structural stability. Johnson & Johnson (13%), American Water Works (6%), Waste Management (7%), and Treasury bonds (5%) anchor the portfolio with companies providing essential community services: healthcare, clean water, sanitation. These defensive cash-generators fund annual 10K “rent” funding during downturns without compromising equity liquidations, It is strategic infrastructure that steward social value while protecting principal.

**(Renamed from Pursue) Construction (50%)** pursues our highest-conviction positioning: infrastructure renovation offers superior risk-adjusted returns aligned with Barwin's mission. PAVE (12%), Xylem (10%), Constellation Energy (8%), Microsoft (8%), and infrastructure specialists represent companies physically rebuilding America's backbone through water systems, energy grids, and digital networks. Therefore, we overweight the stage where lasting civic value gets built.

**(Renamed from Pioneer) Transformation (20%)** pioneers breakthrough growth to elevate the entire portfolio. ASML (7%) monopolizes next-generation semiconductors under AI demand; Eli Lilly (8%) leads the ozempic revolution transforming healthcare. These uncorrelated dominant players in exponential-growth markets provide asymmetric returns that transform solid growth into excellence.

**SECTION 5: REPORT DETAILS**

**A. Investment Strategy.** We started with what felt like an elegant solution: the Greenox Matrix. It was composed of three qualitative lenses mapped into three quantitative sleeves, creating a 3 × 3 evaluation grid that felt comprehensive and intellectually satisfying. Every potential holding could be scored through nine cells, ensuring we covered both financial merit and mission alignment.

The problem? It was too abstract. When we tried explaining it to classmates during a practice presentation, they got lost. More importantly, we got lost. Deciding whether a company scored higher on "Rootedness" or "Resilience" became subjective guesswork.

The real issue emerged during a Saturday Zoom meeting, our third attempt to finalize our investment strategy. We were trying to justify our first major holdings. While Johnson & Johnson fit cleanly into our matrix, teammates questioned our addition of Xylem. "Does it belong in Rootedness because water is essential, or Resilience because utilities are stable, or Reciprocity because it gives back to communities?" Harry asked, genuinely confused. The answer was all three, which meant the categories weren't actually helping us make decisions; instead, they were creating boxes to reverse-engineer justifications.

Nathan pushed harder: "If everything scores high everywhere, what's the point of the framework?" He wasn't wrong. We'd spent three weeks building this Matrix, only to realize we were fooling ourselves with false precision.

Two realizations changed our approach. First, we'd fundamentally misinterpreted Connor Barwin's risk tolerance. We focused on philanthropy and assumed he wanted conservative capital preservation. Our initial allocation reflected this: 50% in "Preserve," 35% in "Pursue," and 15% in "Pioneer."

During our fourth meeting, Ray ran the numbers. To triple $500,000 to $1.5 million over 10 years while withdrawing $10,000 annually starting Year 3, we needed approximately 11-12% annualized returns. Our conservative 50/35/15 allocation historically produced 6-7% returns. We were setting ourselves up to fail Connor's stated objective.

Ray presented these calculations to the team, expecting quick agreement. Instead, he encountered resistance. "But we're managing a nonprofit's money," William argued. "We can't gamble with funds meant for community programs. What if we lose 30% in a market crash?" The 2020 pandemic showed how equity-heavy portfolios could crater. If we took too much risk and failed, we'd potentially deprive Philadelphia neighborhoods of resources they desperately needed.

However, while researching late one night, Ray uncovered an important detail: Connor was a Wharton MBA who co-invested $5 million in an Italian basketball team fighting relegation. That's not the profile of someone afraid of calculated risk. Second, Ray discovered the capex supercycle framework. Grid modernization, AI data centers, water system replacement, electrification: these weren't speculative trends. The American Society of Civil Engineers estimated a $2.6 trillion infrastructure funding gap. Companies like Xylem don't have competitors for upgrading municipal water systems; they are the solution.

This clicked with Barwin's work. He doesn't fund parks abstractly; he renovates undervalued physical infrastructure serving communities for decades. An investment portfolio concentrated in companies renovating America's essential infrastructure mirrors his exact philosophy: patient capital deployed in unavoidable, long-duration projects.

Hence, Ray proposed a controversial reallocation: 30% Foundation, 50% Construction, and 20% Transformation. Our team debated intensely. Nathan argued this violated basic diversification principles. If infrastructure underperforms, we would be significantly exposed. However, this argument convinced the majority: this wasn't sector rotation; it was structural positioning. Every Construction holding solved a problem that must be solved this decade. You cannot run data centers without upgrading electrical grids. You cannot meet EPA standards without replacing century-old pipelines. You cannot handle modern waste volumes without updated logistics infrastructure. We weren't betting these companies would outperform; we were betting America's infrastructure has to get renovated, and these are unavoidable intermediaries.

To address William's concerns, we implemented guardrails: maintaining 3-4 years of distribution obligations in stable assets, rebalancing triggers if Construction exceeded 50%, and quarterly stress tests. The vote was 4-2 in favor.

Later, Harry raised an uncomfortable question: "Are we keeping this Matrix because it helps us make better decisions, or because it makes us look smarter?" The honest answer was the latter. When we actually made investment choices, we didn't reference the Matrix. Darren agreed: "The Matrix creates communication friction. When we present to judges, we'll spend time explaining the framework instead of explaining our holdings."

Therefore, we abandoned the Matrix. Our "Renovation" framework emerged organically. We mirrored how Barwin renovated parks: first, assess the foundation to understand what's structurally sound; second, build the core structure that serves daily needs; third, add transformative features that elevate the whole project. This translated directly to portfolio construction. Foundation holdings (JNJ, AWK, Treasury bonds) provide stability and fund annual distributions. Construction holdings (PAVE, XYL, CEG, WM) capture companies actively building infrastructure America must upgrade this decade. Transformation holdings (ASML, LLY) add breakthrough growth that compounds faster than baseline returns. One question replaced nine cells: "Is this foundational stability, core infrastructure, or transformative growth?" The clarity was immediate.

**B. Team Analysis and Decision-Making.** Before MTWB breaks ground on a park, they survey the site. Before we built a portfolio, we surveyed the market. Our process mirrored Barwin's three-phase approach: Site Assessment (identify structurally sound candidates), Blueprint (confirm they fit the renovation framework), and Inspection (stress-test under adverse conditions).

We identified four sectors where companies physically build and maintain the systems communities depend on: utilities such as electrical grids and water systems, clean energy including nuclear baseload and renewable generation, healthcare through essential medicines and medical devices, and digital infrastructure through cloud software and semiconductors. Connor spends $2.5 million renovating Smith Playground because it serves South Philadelphia for more than 50 years. That is patient capital invested in infrastructure with long-term community utility. We applied the same logic to equities: Utilities such as American Water Works maintain municipal water systems; Clean energy providers like Constellation Energy supply reliable baseload power for data centers; Healthcare firms like Johnson & Johnson manufacture essential products used daily in communities worldwide; Digital infrastructure companies enable the commerce and communication modern communities rely on. These are not speculative growth bets on new technologies; They are equity stakes in the physical and operational systems communities cannot function without.

**Site Assessment.** We analyzed 70 tickers using metrics from Yahoo Finance and FactSet across three dimensions [2]: risk (5-year volatility, maximum drawdown, beta), return (EBITDA, ROIC-WACC spread, free cash flow yield), and mission alignment (employee ownership, capital expenditure intensity, Sustainalytics ESG scores). This shortlisted 30 structurally sound candidates.

**Blueprint.** Site assessment tells you what's stable, but it doesn't tell you what to build. The Blueprint phase asked: does this company renovate essential infrastructure America must upgrade this decade?

Johnson & Johnson illustrates this. JNJ passed site assessment with stable cash flows, 60+ years of consecutive dividend increases, and low volatility (beta 0.52). But the Blueprint question was different: does it align with Connor's mission? JNJ manufactures essential healthcare products globally, operates with long-term stewardship culture, and generates 3.1% dividend yield supporting MTWB's $10,000 annual distribution. Financially sound companies are common, but companies that align cleanly with the mission are not.

Each company that passed both filters was assigned to Foundation (stability and distributions), Construction (active infrastructure building), or Transformation (breakthrough growth catalysts).

**Inspection.** You don't break ground without knowing the structure can handle storms. We stress-tested the portfolio using the 2020 pandemic drawdown (-34% peak-to-trough), 2008 financial crisis scenarios, and hypothetical infrastructure spending stalls. We focused on three practical questions: Could the portfolio sustain MTWB's $10,000 annual distribution during downturns? Could we avoid forced selling during stress periods? Could we preserve our ability to compound toward $1.5M over the full 10-year horizon?

The structure held. Despite deliberate concentration in infrastructure holdings, defensive Foundation positions provided liquidity and income during stress periods, while Construction holdings introduced volatility but preserved upside during recoveries. The goal wasn't eliminating drawdowns but managing sequence risk: ensuring temporary declines wouldn't force us to liquidate growth positions at the worst possible time, thereby protecting our long-term compounding trajectory.

**C. Team Growth and Learning.** Our team began like most new investment groups: smart individuals moving fast, but not necessarily moving together. In the first weeks, collaboration looked productive. Everyone researched names, sent charts, and pitched ideas. However, we had no shared standard for when an idea was truly ready to become a trade. The portfolio began to reflect our internal noise rather than a unified decision system.

The turning point was painful and clarifying. We placed a trade that should never have happened, and it cost us real money. Our FMC position fell 54 percent on a $10,000 allocation, resulting in a $5,400 loss. What concerned us more was how it happened. Nobody intended to be careless. We simply lacked a system that could stop a rushed decision from becoming an executed trade.

Ray called an emergency meeting to redesign how we made decisions. The Saturday Zoom call was uncomfortable. Ray opened by accepting responsibility, stating that approving FMC without proper research was a leadership failure. Others admitted their own gaps. We agreed on a new rule: no trade without a written memo and two independent confirmations. Every proposal now required a one-page investment note covering rationale, key risks, mission alignment, and exit triggers. A second teammate was assigned to challenge the idea.

The change was not instant, but it was real. Two weeks later, Harry pitched Eli Lilly with thorough research. William red-teamed the idea and identified meaningful regulatory risks. We voted 5-0, waited 24 hours, and executed. By Week 10, Eli Lilly became our best performer, up 18 percent. Disagreements stopped feeling personal because dissent now had a purpose: to stress-test ideas before the market did. We stopped acting like solo builders and began operating like a coordinated construction crew.

**D. Challenges and Roadblocks.** The FMC loss forced us to confront a harder truth: we'd been treating research as individual work rather than team validation. After implementing our new process, we faced a second test during Week 7 when contradictory earnings guidance from two Construction holdings (Xylem and Waste Management) triggered a broader sector selloff. Despite our immediate instinct was to reduce exposure, we followed our new protocol: Nathan stress-tested whether the selloff reflected fundamental deterioration or temporary sentiment; William reviewed guidance transcripts to separate noise from signal; Harry analyzed whether municipal infrastructure budgets actually changed. The answer was no on all fronts. We held our positions, increased cash reserves slightly to maintain distribution coverage, and avoided panic selling. The sector recovered within three weeks, which validated that our process wasn't just about preventing bad trades but about making disciplined decisions under pressure.

**E. Biggest Takeaways.** As students, we learned that elegant frameworks are not always effective frameworks. The Greenox Matrix looked impressive but became a tool for reverse-engineering justifications. The breakthrough came when we asked simpler questions: "Does this company build infrastructure that America must upgrade?" Clarity beats complexity.

As teammates, we learned that collaboration requires infrastructure just like cities do. We introduced weekly rotating roles to ensure depth of analysis and prevent groupthink. One member tracked macro conditions, one focused on fundamentals, one monitored portfolio risk, one documented decisions, one executed trades only after confirmations. The hardest lesson was accepting group responsibility when one person made a mistake. Blame fractures teams; accountability strengthens them.

As money managers, we learned that risk tolerance is mathematical before it becomes emotional. We initially framed Barwin as conservative due to MTWB's community funding obligations. However, tripling $500,000 to $1.5 million over ten years requires 11-12% annualized returns. Our original conservative allocation could not achieve that. This reframed our mandate: build a portfolio capable of exceeding the target while protecting annual funding needs. Sometimes the most conservative choice is being aggressive enough to overdeliver on the objective.

As future leaders, we learned that conviction requires concentration, but concentration requires homework. Our 50% Construction allocation violated every diversification rule we'd been taught. One teammate called it reckless. What convinced us wasn't confidence; it was research. The American Society of Civil Engineers' $2.6 trillion infrastructure gap isn't a prediction; it's deferred maintenance coming due. Companies like Xylem and Waste Management aren't speculative growth plays; they're unavoidable intermediaries when century-old water systems must be replaced. The real leadership lesson is defending an unpopular position, but earning that conviction through work, not just enthusiasm.

END OF FINAL REPORT

**Advisor.** Over the past ten weeks, it has been a rewarding experience to witness the growth of the Greenox team through their participation in the Wharton Global High School Investment Competition. This has enabled them to develop a deeper understanding of investing principles and the complexities of investment management. Importantly, they grew as collaborators and as a team - sharing ideas, debating strategies, and making data-driven decisions as a team. As Warren Buffett wisely said, “The best investment you can make is in yourself.”, the Greenox team have truly invested in their own learning. **Team.** Greenox aims to think like a philanthropist by using our strengths in research, communication, and finance to expand opportunities for young people in our own community, starting with our own school. Inspired by Connor Barwin's focus on long-term community-led change, we want to address a specific gap: many of our peers lack practical experience managing money or understanding investment basics. Through this competition, we've learned to analyze companies, build portfolios, and think strategically about long-term goals, which are skills that aren't typically taught in our Australian curriculum. We envision creating accessible peer-to-peer workshops where students can explore financial concepts through hands-on activities like portfolio simulations or conversations with local business owners. By keeping these initiatives student-led and community-centered, we can create something sustainable that grows based on what our peers actually need. **SECTION 2: ADVISOR AND TEAM REFLECTIONS**

**Advisor.** Over the past ten weeks, it has been a rewarding experience to witness the growth of the Greenox team through their participation in the Wharton Global High School Investment Competition. The Greenox team engaged in rigorous research, portfolio analysis, and collaborative decision-making throughout their investing journey. This has enabled them to develop a deeper understanding of investing principles and the complexities of investment management. Importantly, they grew as collaborators and as a team - sharing ideas, debating strategies, and making data-driven decisions as a team. As Warren Buffett wisely said, “The best investment you can make is in yourself.”, the Greenox team have truly invested in their own learning. Watching their transformation from curious learners to informed decision-makers has been inspiring.

**Team.** Inspired by Connor Barwin’s focus on long-term community-led change, we want to apply what this competition taught us to address a gap at our own Australian school, where many peers lack practical experience managing money. Managing Connor’s portfolio showed us that investment decisions carry real consequences. Our FMC loss cost $5,400, capital that could have funded half a year of MTWB distributions, and forced us to professionalize through written memos, red-teaming, and cooling periods. We learned to think beyond quarterly returns by focusing on sequence risk. Could the portfolio withstand a 2008-level crisis without forced liquidation to fund annual distributions? This shift from theoretical optimization to practical stewardship is what we aim to share through peer-led workshops and portfolio simulations. Managing real capital for a real mission taught us that investing is not about elegant frameworks, but about building portfolios that accomplish objectives under real-world constraints.

**SECTION 3: TRADING NOTE ANALYSIS** 

**(ETF) BATS: PAVE (Global X US Infrastructure Development ETF)**[image]

**Reflection:** PAVE provided diversified exposure to the U.S. infrastructure capex cycle, translating a broad political and economic theme into a disciplined portfolio position. We used PAVE to hardwire our “Renovation” philosophy into the portfolio, gaining exposure to roads, grids, utilities, and industrial assets without relying on single-stock timing. Its 99 holdings across construction materials, heavy machinery and engineering reduced concentration risk, which was critical given infrastructure’s dependence on multi-year funding cycles. While infrastructure spending can be cyclical, PAVE’s breadth allows us to express long-term conviction in physical renewal while mitigating regulatory and execution risk inherent in individual names.

**(Stock) NYSE: JNJ (Johnson and Johnson)**

[image]

JNJ taught us that resilient infrastructure is not only physical, but human. Healthcare emerged as essential “human infrastructure,” particularly as aging populations drive sustained demand for medical services. JNJ’s low volatility profile (beta 0.52) and long dividend history made it a Foundation holding designed to stabilize the portfolio during market stress. Its 3.1% dividend yield supported MTWB’s $10,000 annual distributions, reducing the likelihood of forced equity sales during downturns. Pairing JNJ with more cyclical infrastructure names reinforced that stewardship requires balancing growth with reliability, ensuring capital remains productive even when markets are unstable.

**(Team Choice) NYSE: XYL (Xylem Inc)**

Reflection:[image]

Xylem made our strategy tangible by linking investment decisions directly to public infrastructure that affects daily life. Its focus on water systems aligned closely with MTWB’s stormwater and resilience initiatives, making it a high-conviction Construction holding. The premium valuation forced us to debate growth versus price discipline, but we concluded that Xylem’s position in a replacement-driven water infrastructure cycle justified the trade-off. Municipal water systems face decades of deferred maintenance, and Xylem operates as a critical intermediary with limited substitutes. This decision reinforced that mission alignment alone is insufficient; it must be supported by durable demand and long-term capital deployment.

**SECTION 5: REPORT DETAILS**

**A. Investment Strategy.** We started with what felt like an elegant solution: the Greenox Matrix. It was composed of three qualitative lenses mapped into three quantitative sleeves, creating a 3 × 3 evaluation grid that felt comprehensive and intellectually satisfying. Every potential holding could be scored through nine cells, ensuring we covered both financial merit and mission alignment.

The problem? It was too abstract. When we tried explaining it to classmates during a practice presentation, they got lost. More importantly, we got lost. Deciding whether a company scored higher on "Rootedness" or "Resilience" became subjective guesswork.

The real issue emerged during a Saturday Zoom meeting, our third attempt to finalize our investment strategy. We were trying to justify our first major holdings. While Johnson & Johnson fit cleanly into our matrix, teammates questioned our addition of Xylem. "Does it belong in Rootedness because water is essential, or Resilience because utilities are stable, or Reciprocity because it gives back to communities?" Harry asked, genuinely confused. The answer was all three, which meant the categories weren't actually helping us make decisions; instead, they were creating boxes to reverse-engineer justifications.

Nathan pushed harder: "If everything scores high everywhere, what's the point of the framework?" He wasn't wrong. We'd spent three weeks building this Matrix, only to realize we were fooling ourselves with false precision.

Two realizations changed our approach. First, we'd fundamentally misinterpreted Connor Barwin's risk tolerance. We focused on philanthropy and assumed he wanted conservative capital preservation. Our initial allocation reflected this: 50% in "Preserve," 35% in "Pursue," and 15% in "Pioneer."

During our fourth meeting, Ray ran the numbers. To triple $500,000 to $1.5 million over 10 years while withdrawing $10,000 annually starting Year 3, we needed approximately 11-12% annualized returns. Our conservative 50/35/15 allocation historically produced 6-7% returns. We were setting ourselves up to fail Connor's stated objective.

Ray presented these calculations to the team, expecting quick agreement. Instead, he encountered resistance. "But we're managing a nonprofit's money," William argued. "We can't gamble with funds meant for community programs. What if we lose 30% in a market crash?" The 2020 pandemic showed how equity-heavy portfolios could crater. If we took too much risk and failed, we'd potentially deprive Philadelphia neighborhoods of resources they desperately needed.

However, while researching late one night, Ray uncovered an important detail: Connor was a Wharton MBA who co-invested $5 million in an Italian basketball team fighting relegation. That's not the profile of someone afraid of calculated risk. Second, Ray discovered the capex supercycle framework. Grid modernization, AI data centers, water system replacement, electrification: these weren't speculative trends. The American Society of Civil Engineers estimated a $2.6 trillion infrastructure funding gap. Companies like Xylem don't have competitors for upgrading municipal water systems; they are the solution.

This clicked with Barwin's work. He doesn't fund parks abstractly; he renovates undervalued physical infrastructure serving communities for decades. An investment portfolio concentrated in companies renovating America's essential infrastructure mirrors his exact philosophy: patient capital deployed in unavoidable, long-duration projects.

Hence, Ray proposed a controversial reallocation: 30% Foundation, 50% Construction, and 20% Transformation. Our team debated intensely. Nathan argued this violated basic diversification principles. If infrastructure underperforms, we would be significantly exposed. However, this argument convinced the majority: this wasn't sector rotation; it was structural positioning. Every Construction holding solved a problem that must be solved this decade. You cannot run data centers without upgrading electrical grids. You cannot meet EPA standards without replacing century-old pipelines. You cannot handle modern waste volumes without updated logistics infrastructure. We weren't betting these companies would outperform; we were betting America's infrastructure has to get renovated, and these are unavoidable intermediaries.

To address William's concerns, we implemented guardrails: maintaining 3-4 years of distribution obligations in stable assets, rebalancing triggers if Construction exceeded 50%, and quarterly stress tests. The vote was 4-2 in favor.

Later, Harry raised an uncomfortable question: "Are we keeping this Matrix because it helps us make better decisions, or because it makes us look smarter?" The honest answer was the latter. When we actually made investment choices, we didn't reference the Matrix. Darren agreed: "The Matrix creates communication friction. When we present to judges, we'll spend time explaining the framework instead of explaining our holdings."

Therefore, we abandoned the Matrix. Our "Renovation" framework emerged organically. We mirrored how Barwin renovated parks: first, assess the foundation to understand what's structurally sound; second, build the core structure that serves daily needs; third, add transformative features that elevate the whole project. This translated directly to portfolio construction. Foundation holdings (JNJ, AWK, Treasury bonds) provide stability and fund annual distributions. Construction holdings (PAVE, XYL, CEG, WM) capture companies actively building infrastructure America must upgrade this decade. Transformation holdings (ASML, LLY) add breakthrough growth that compounds faster than baseline returns. One question replaced nine cells: "Is this foundational stability, core infrastructure, or transformative growth?" The clarity was immediate.

**B. Team Analysis and Decision-Making.** Before MTWB breaks ground on a park, they survey the site. Before we built a portfolio, we surveyed the market. Our process mirrored Barwin's three-phase approach: Site Assessment (identify structurally sound candidates), Blueprint (confirm they fit the renovation framework), and Inspection (stress-test under adverse conditions).

We identified four sectors where companies physically build and maintain the systems communities depend on: utilities such as electrical grids and water systems, clean energy including nuclear baseload and renewable generation, healthcare through essential medicines and medical devices, and digital infrastructure through cloud software and semiconductors. Connor spends $2.5 million renovating Smith Playground because it serves South Philadelphia for more than 50 years. That is patient capital invested in infrastructure with long-term community utility. We applied the same logic to equities: Utilities such as American Water Works maintain municipal water systems; Clean energy providers like Constellation Energy supply reliable baseload power for data centers; Healthcare firms like Johnson & Johnson manufacture essential products used daily in communities worldwide; Digital infrastructure companies enable the commerce and communication modern communities rely on. These are not speculative growth bets on new technologies; They are equity stakes in the physical and operational systems communities cannot function without.

**Site Assessment.** We analyzed 70 tickers using metrics from Yahoo Finance and FactSet across three dimensions: risk (5-year volatility, maximum drawdown, beta), return (EBITDA, ROIC-WACC spread, free cash flow yield), and mission alignment (employee ownership, capital expenditure intensity, Sustainalytics ESG scores). This shortlisted 30 structurally sound candidates.

**Blueprint.** Site assessment tells you what's stable, but it doesn't tell you what to build. The Blueprint phase asked: does this company renovate essential infrastructure America must upgrade this decade?

Johnson & Johnson illustrates this. JNJ passed site assessment with stable cash flows, 60+ years of consecutive dividend increases, and low volatility (beta 0.52). But the Blueprint question was different: does it align with Connor's mission? JNJ manufactures essential healthcare products globally, operates with long-term stewardship culture, and generates 3.1% dividend yield supporting MTWB's $10,000 annual distribution. Financially sound companies are common, but companies that align cleanly with the mission are not.

Each company that passed both filters was assigned to Foundation (stability and distributions), Construction (active infrastructure building), or Transformation (breakthrough growth catalysts).

**Inspection.** You don't break ground without knowing the structure can handle storms. We stress-tested the portfolio using the 2020 pandemic drawdown (-34% peak-to-trough), 2008 financial crisis scenarios, and hypothetical infrastructure spending stalls. We focused on three practical questions: Could the portfolio sustain MTWB's $10,000 annual distribution during downturns? Could we avoid forced selling during stress periods? Could we preserve our ability to compound toward $1.5M over the full 10-year horizon?

The structure held. Despite deliberate concentration in infrastructure holdings, defensive Foundation positions provided liquidity and income during stress periods, while Construction holdings introduced volatility but preserved upside during recoveries. The goal wasn't eliminating drawdowns but managing sequence risk: ensuring temporary declines wouldn't force us to liquidate growth positions at the worst possible time, thereby protecting our long-term compounding trajectory.

**C. Team Growth and Learning.** Our team began like most new investment groups: smart individuals moving fast, but not necessarily moving together. In the first weeks, collaboration looked productive. Everyone researched names, sent charts, and pitched ideas. However, we had no shared standard for when an idea was truly ready to become a trade. The portfolio began to reflect our internal noise rather than a unified decision system.

The turning point was painful and clarifying. We placed a trade that should never have happened, and it cost us real money. Our FMC position fell 54 percent on a $10,000 allocation, resulting in a $5,400 loss. What concerned us more was how it happened. Nobody intended to be careless. We simply lacked a system that could stop a rushed decision from becoming an executed trade.

Ray called an emergency meeting to redesign how we made decisions. The Saturday Zoom call was uncomfortable. Ray opened by accepting responsibility, stating that approving FMC without proper research was a leadership failure. Others admitted their own gaps. We agreed on a new rule: no trade without a written memo and two independent confirmations. Every proposal now required a one-page investment note covering rationale, key risks, mission alignment, and exit triggers. A second teammate was assigned to challenge the idea.

The change was not instant, but it was real. Two weeks later, Harry pitched Eli Lilly with thorough research. William red-teamed the idea and identified meaningful regulatory risks. We voted 5-0, waited 24 hours, and executed. By Week 10, Eli Lilly became our best performer, up 18 percent. Disagreements stopped feeling personal because dissent now had a purpose: to stress-test ideas before the market did. We stopped acting like solo builders and began operating like a coordinated construction crew.

**D. Challenges and Roadblocks.** The FMC loss forced us to confront a harder truth: we'd been treating research as individual work rather than team validation. After implementing our new process, we faced a second test during Week 7 when contradictory earnings guidance from two Construction holdings (Xylem and Waste Management) triggered a broader sector selloff. Despite our immediate instinct was to reduce exposure, we followed our new protocol: Nathan stress-tested whether the selloff reflected fundamental deterioration or temporary sentiment; William reviewed guidance transcripts to separate noise from signal; Harry analyzed whether municipal infrastructure budgets actually changed. The answer was no on all fronts. We held our positions, increased cash reserves slightly to maintain distribution coverage, and avoided panic selling. The sector recovered within three weeks, which validated that our process wasn't just about preventing bad trades but about making disciplined decisions under pressure.

**E. Biggest Takeaways.** As students, we learned that elegant frameworks are not always effective frameworks. The Greenox Matrix looked impressive but became a tool for reverse-engineering justifications. The breakthrough came when we asked simpler questions: "Does this company build infrastructure that America must upgrade?" Clarity beats complexity.

As teammates, we learned that collaboration requires infrastructure just like cities do. We introduced weekly rotating roles to ensure depth of analysis and prevent groupthink. One member tracked macro conditions, one focused on fundamentals, one monitored portfolio risk, one documented decisions, one executed trades only after confirmations. The hardest lesson was accepting group responsibility when one person made a mistake. Blame fractures teams; accountability strengthens them.

As money managers, we learned that risk tolerance is mathematical before it becomes emotional. We initially framed Barwin as conservative due to MTWB's community funding obligations. However, tripling $500,000 to $1.5 million over ten years requires 11-12% annualized returns. Our original conservative allocation could not achieve that. This reframed our mandate: build a portfolio capable of exceeding the target while protecting annual funding needs. Sometimes the most conservative choice is being aggressive enough to overdeliver on the objective.

As future leaders, we learned that conviction requires concentration, but concentration requires homework. Our 50% Construction allocation violated every diversification rule we'd been taught. One teammate called it reckless. What convinced us wasn't confidence; it was research. The American Society of Civil Engineers' $2.6 trillion infrastructure gap isn't a prediction; it's deferred maintenance coming due. Companies like Xylem and Waste Management aren't speculative growth plays; they're unavoidable intermediaries when century-old water systems must be replaced. The real leadership lesson is defending an unpopular position, but earning that conviction through work, not just enthusiasm.

## Tab: CORE STRATEGY

_Tab ID: `t.9bgidpaqm07k`_

### **Greenox Core Investment Strategy**

#### **Core Objective**

Greenox manages capital with the same mindset MTWB applies to urban revitalization: treating the portfolio as a **renovation project** that must appreciate significantly in value while serving the community daily. Our mandate is to engineer an aggressive growth trajectory to hit the $1.5M target, while structurally generating a **self-sustaining "rent"** (yield) to cover the $10,000 annual draw without eroding principal. We prioritize "Builder" companies—firms that physically improve cities and infrastructure—ensuring the portfolio is not just a financial asset, but a mirror of Barwin’s work in the physical world.

#### **Signature 1:  The Greenox Matrix**

Greenox expands the classic Markowitz risk–return model into a fully three-dimensional geometry that incorporates mission alignment as an essential axis of evaluation. 

##### **X: Risk**

Evaluates financial strength and stability using quantitative indicators such as volatility, maximum drawdown and 5 year beta. This dimension ensures companies can withstand economic cycles while protecting MTWB’s liquidity needs.

##### **Y: Return**

Assesses long-term value creation by examining EBITDA, ROIC–WACC value spread and Free Cashflow Yield. This identifies businesses capable of compounding capital sustainably over a decade-long horizon to fit Barwin’s goals

##### **Z: ESG**

Measures' missions fit across the ESG, which include the CapEx, Sustainalytics ESG Risk Score and Employee Ownership Percentage.

*For each axis the item will be scored between [0,1] based on its normalized score.* 

#### **Signature 2: Point-Line-Plane and Stewardship Surface**

The point, line plane structure reciprocates the Architectural Design / Blueprint. We define the following elements:

| **Element** | **Investment Concept** | **Definition** |
|---|---|---|
| **Point** | Stock Candidates | Each firm’s location is defined by its distinct (X,Y,Z) coordinates based on quantitative data gathering |
| **Line** | Quantitative Metrics | Three normalized axes as explained above. |
| **Plane** | Qualitative Evaluation and Stewardship Surface | The Pareto Frontier of purpose and performance, showing the optimal trade-offs. |

The **Stewardship Surface** is the boundary where no portfolio can achieve higher Return and higher Stewardship with the same or lower Risk. Points below the surface are dominated and represent a measurable opportunity cost relative to the client’s values.

Example model that can be pasted on our final report:  
  
[image]

#### **Portfolio Architecture: The 3P Sleeves**

---
Greenox integrates the Matrix into a portfolio design using three sleeves, each reflecting a distinct role in the architecture of long-term stewardship.

##### **Preserve (≈50%): Stability and Funding Base**

*Low Risk, Moderate return, High ESG.*

Composed of essential, low-volatility companies with stable dividend profiles. These holdings support MTWB’s annual $10K draw and function as the stabilizing anchor of the portfolio. Typical exposures include regulated utilities, water infrastructure, renewable operators, and essential service providers.

##### **Pursue (≈35%): Quality Growth Engine**

*Balanced Trade-offs and Quality Growth*

Dedicated to high-quality compounders with strong competitive advantages, recurring revenue models, durable margins, and long-term secular tailwinds. This sleeve contains global healthcare leaders, premier industrial innovators, and consumer essentials companies that can reliably scale earnings while maintaining high returns on invested capital.

##### **Pioneer (≈15%): Future Stewardship Engines**

*Higher Risk, Higher Return and feature* ***emerging stewardship.*** 

Focused on emerging yet structurally important sectors that advance community resilience and long-term societal value. These include clean energy grids, electrification platforms, semiconductor leaders, next-generation materials, and climate-aligned infrastructure. This sleeve introduces controlled asymmetry—measured risk paired with steep long-term potential.

*Focus on* ***Local Innovation*** *and* ***Green Tech.***

#### **Market Rationale**

  
We are entering a "Capex Supercycle" driven by three forces that align with MTWB:

- **Re-Industrialization:** Bringing manufacturing back requires massive infrastructure spend (Good for our Industrial picks).
- **The Electrification of Everything:** The grid needs to be rebuilt to handle AI and EVs (Good for our Copper/Grid picks).
- **Urban Resilience:** Cities must upgrade water and transit systems to survive climate change (Good for our Water/Transit picks).
*Conclusion:* We aren't just betting on stock prices; we are betting on the physical rebuilding of the American economy.

## Tab: CORE STRATEGY > Portfolio Design

_Tab ID: `t.tfo5qfnvvr6b`_

### **‘**

### **Portfolio Design**

**Client:** Connor Barwin / MTWB Foundation **Objective:** Triple capital ($500k $\to$ $1.5M) by 2036 while generating a sustainable $10k annual community yield.

##### **1. Our Philosophy:** ***Investing as Renovation******.***

Most foundations treat capital passively. Greenox treats this portfolio like a **physical renovation project**. We reject generic ESG scores in favor of a **"Builder Mindset,"** allocating capital to companies that physically construct, power, and heal the communities MTWB serves.

- **The "Moneyball" Approach:** We utilize a 3D Matrix (Risk, Return, Stewardship) to identify inefficiencies. We favor **High-CapEx** companies (those spending money to build assets) over "asset-light" companies that simply buy back stock.
- **The Math:** To achieve the $1.5M target with annual withdrawals, we require a **~12.5% CAGR**. This necessitates a shift from low-growth Utilities to high-growth Infrastructure & Tech.

##### **2. Thematic Architecture**

We reject rigid GICS industry codes. Instead, we map assets into **4 Impact Themes** that mirror the components of a functional city:

| **THEME** | **DEFINITION** | **KEY HOLDINGS** |
|---|---|---|
| **Sustainable Infrastructure** | **The Foundation:** The metabolic systems of the city—water, waste, transit, and materials. | **VMC** (Vulcan Materials), **XYL** (Xylem), **WM** (Waste Management), **WAB** (Wabtec), **PAVE** (Infra ETF), **HD** (Home Depot) |
| **Energy Transition** | **The Power:** Generation and transmission hardware for the electrified future. | **GEV** (GE Vernova), **NEE** (NextEra Energy), **SU** (Schneider Electric), **FSLR** (First Solar) |
| **Healthcare** | **The People:** A holistic approach to health, spanning preventative youth activity to therapeutics. | **LLY** (Eli Lilly), **DXCM** (Dexcom), **JNJ** (Johnson & Johnson) |
| **Digital Infrastructure** | **The Economy:** The compute power and connectivity required for a modern AI economy. | **NVDA** (NVIDIA), **MSFT** (Microsoft), **ASML** (ASML), **TSM** (TSMC) |

##### **3. PORTFOLIO CONSTRUCTION (THE 3P SLEEVES)**

To balance the aggressive growth target with the immediate liquidity need, we structure the holdings into three distinct roles:

**I. PRESERVE (~30%): The Yield Floor**

- **Role:** Generates the $10,000 annual withdrawal via natural yield (Dividends) so we never sell principal during a downturn.
- **Strategy:** Replaced low-growth water utilities (AWK) with "Growth Utilities" and essential services.
- **Anchors:** **JNJ** (AAA Stability), **NEE** (Green Utility), **WM** (Circular Economy Utility).
**II. PURSUE (~50%): The Builders (Core Compounders)**

- **Role:** The engine of the portfolio. Dominant oligopolies with tangible community impact.
- **Strategy:** Companies that provide the physical building blocks of society.
- **Anchors:** **VMC** (Concrete), **HD** (Renovation Supply), **LLY** (Metabolic Health), **DKS** (Youth Sports), **SU** (Smart Buildings).
**III. PIONEER (~20%): The Alpha (Venture Growth)**

- **Role:** The aggressive growth required to hit the 12.5% CAGR target.
- **Strategy:** "Hard Tech" infrastructure and high-conviction moonshots.
- **Anchors:** **NVDA** (AI Infra), **GEV** (Grid Expansion), **FSLR** (US Manufacturing), **ABAT** (Circular Battery Tech).
**The Result:** A portfolio that is no longer a collection of stocks, but a **blueprint for urban renewal.**


## Tab: MACRO ANALYSIS

_Tab ID: `t.qsrdrt30pv47`_

### **Macroeconomic Analysis: US Economy in 2025 and Investment Implications**

#### **Executive Summary**

The US economy in 2025 presents a complex and contradictory macroeconomic landscape characterized by resilient headline growth metrics conflicting sharply with deteriorating underlying fundamentals. While third-quarter real GDP growth of 3.6% and year-to-date technology sector gains of 22% suggest continued economic momentum, deeper analysis reveals significant headwinds: resurging inflation, labor market weakening, precipitous declines in investor confidence regarding artificial intelligence valuations, and substantial drag from expansive tariff policies. This divergence between surface-level data and underlying vulnerabilities creates substantial portfolio risks requiring strategic repositioning.

For the Wharton Investment Competition context, this macro backdrop is critical for client advisory—Connor Barwin, as a business executive with substantial asset base and long-term wealth preservation goals, would require a portfolio strategy that balances growth aspirations against mounting macro uncertainties.

---

#### **Core Macroeconomic Conditions**

#### **Inflation Remains Sticky Above Federal Reserve Target**

Inflation accelerated notably through 2025, with the Consumer Price Index rising from a stable 2.7% annually in the first half of the year to 3.0% by September, maintaining that elevated level through November. This represents the highest inflation reading since January 2025, exceeding the Federal Reserve's 2% target by a significant margin. The stickiness is particularly concerning because it contradicts the disinflationary trajectory anticipated earlier in the year.​

US Inflation Trend (CPI Annual Rate) - 2025

Key Inflation Drivers:

- Energy prices surged, with gasoline rising 4.1% month-on-month in September, contributing substantially to headline inflation​
- Food prices increased significantly alongside broader price pressures​
- Housing costs, while showing some signs of moderation, remain elevated at 3.5-3.8% annually​
- Core inflation remained remarkably stable at 3.1%, suggesting persistent underlying price pressures​
The Federal Reserve faces a dilemma: inflation remains 50 basis points above their 2% target while labor market weakness suggests economic deceleration. Fed officials remain divided, with some concerned that tariffs could push inflation further above target, while others worry that the jobs market is cooling too rapidly.​

#### **Labor Market Deterioration Accelerates**

The employment picture darkened significantly through 2025, contradicting assumptions of a soft landing. The unemployment rate climbed from 4.1% in June to 4.3% by August—the highest level since October 2021—and remained at that elevated level through October. This 20-basis-point increase represents meaningful labor market slack developing after years of tight conditions.​

US Unemployment Rate vs Federal Funds Rate - 2025

Labor Market Concerns:

- Unemployment growth trajectory: 4.1% (June) → 4.2% (July) → 4.3% (August, September, October)​
- Unemployed persons increased by 148,000 to 7.384 million in August alone​
- Federal Reserve Bank of New York data revealed employee anxiety spiking, with perceived unemployment probability one year forward reaching 43% in October—up three consecutive months​
- Layoff patterns accelerated, with weak jobs data preceding the technology sector volatility​
The deteriorating labor market provided the rationale for the Federal Reserve to cut rates by 25 basis points in both September and October, bringing the federal funds rate from 4.33% to 3.875%—the lowest level since 2022. However, policymakers explicitly warned that a third rate cut in December is "not a forgone conclusion...far from it."​

#### **Consumer Confidence Collapses to Multi-Year Lows**

Perhaps most alarming is the precipitous decline in consumer sentiment, which collapsed to 50.3 in November—the lowest reading since June 2022—amid the federal government shutdown. This represents a staggering drop of nearly 30% year-over-year and marks three consecutive months of deterioration.​

Sentiment Dynamics:

- Conference Board Consumer Confidence Index declined to 94.6 in October, down 1.0 point, with the "Expectations Index" remaining below the recession-warning threshold of 80 since February 2025​
- University of Michigan survey showed consumers expressing substantial pessimism about personal finances (down 17%) and future business conditions (down 11%)​
- K-shaped economy dynamics evident: high-income households with stock holdings showed 11% sentiment improvement due to market strength, while lower-income households faced challenges​
This bifurcation reflects the stark wealth gap implications of market dynamics—equity holder sentiment remains resilient while labor participants and lower-income households grow increasingly pessimistic.

---

#### **The Unraveling AI Boom and Technology Sector Vulnerability**

#### **Market Rotation Away from "Magnificent 7" Technology Stocks**

After dominating market returns through most of 2025, technology stocks experienced a dramatic correction beginning in late October, revealing underlying valuation concerns and investor overextension. The Morningstar US Technology Index fell 5.6% from its October 29 peak, with the worst week (4.5% decline) since April's tariff-induced selloff. Within technology, the declines were far more severe:​

Technology Sector Performance - 2025

- Nvidia, the semiconductor bellwether, dropped 9.1% in a single week and faced sustained selling pressure​
- AMD declined 7% in recent sessions​
- Tesla and Palantir each fell more than 5%​
- Broadcom and Intel saw declines exceeding 3%​
Despite these pullbacks, technology stocks remain up 22% year-to-date, but the underlying dynamics have shifted decisively away from the earlier "AI everything" momentum that powered gains through October.

#### **AI Valuation Bubble Concerns Intensify**

The recent technology selloff is fundamentally rooted in deteriorating investor confidence in artificial intelligence business models and return trajectories. Research from Bank of America's October 2025 Fund Manager Survey found that 54% of institutional investors now characterize AI assets as being in bubble territory, with 60% declaring equities broadly overvalued. These same managers have reduced cash allocations to 3.8%, leaving little dry powder for further market shocks.​

Valuation Red Flags:

- OpenAI CEO Sam Altman stated in August that "some valuations are insane"​
- JPMorgan CEO Jamie Dimon warned of a "serious market correction within two years"​
- IMF officials flagged systemic risks tied to speculative technology themes​
- Elevated Treasury yields keep discount rates high, amplifying valuation stress on growth stocks​
Morningstar analyst Mark Hackett interprets the weakness as reflecting "increasing skepticism over the AI narrative, or concerns over valuations," while noting that the most speculative names—including quantum computing stocks and meme stocks—had already faltered before core technology stocks began to decline. This suggests that last week's weakness in mega-cap names indicates exhaustion of risk appetite rather than selective rotation.​

#### **Near-Term Catalysts for Continued Tech Volatility**

Multiple earnings announcements and macro developments will test AI bulls' thesis:

- Nvidia earnings later in November will test demand narratives​
- Non-farm payroll data will confirm or refute weak job reports​
- Any further Fed guidance shifts will immediately affect discount models valuing growth companies​
- Potential new chip export controls could trigger additional technology sector declines​

---

#### **Tariff-Driven Economic Headwinds**

#### **Substantial Revenue Raised, Substantial Economic Cost**

The Trump administration's tariff policies have generated remarkable revenue—$28 billion in June 2025 alone, triple the monthly average from 2024. Over the decade from 2026-2035, the Congressional Budget Office estimates tariffs will generate $2.5 trillion in government revenue (or $2.0 trillion net of economic effects). However, this fiscal benefit comes at a severe economic cost:​

Tariff Impact on US Economy - 2025 Forecast

Tariff Impact Estimates (Yale Budget Lab Analysis):

- Consumer Price Impact: +1.3% short-run increase in price level, equivalent to average household income loss of $1,800 in 2025$​
- GDP Growth Drag: -0.5 percentage points annually over 2025-2026, with persistent -0.4% long-run contraction​
- Unemployment: Rises 0.3pp by end-2025 and 0.7pp by end-2026​
- Employment Loss: 490,000 fewer jobs by end-2025​
- Trade Effect: Exports contract -15% in long run​
The tariff structure also creates sectoral reallocation, with manufacturing output expanding 2.5% long-run while construction output contracts 3.8% and agriculture declines 0.3%. These are not small adjustments—they represent substantial wealth destruction and labor market disruption.​

#### **Tariff Pass-Through and Consumer Burden**

The economic impact is fundamentally regressive. The Yale Budget Lab analysis assumes full tariff pass-through to consumers if the Federal Reserve does not react to tariffs—meaning price adjustments rather than wage adjustments bear the burden. However, since real income adjustment must occur, and lower-income households spend a substantially larger fraction of income on consumption than higher-income households, the burden falls disproportionately on middle and lower-income Americans.​

Market observers note that inflation fears remain "overdone" due to slow tariff pass-through and disinflationary pressures from lower energy costs, slowing housing rents, and cooling wage growth—but this remains an uncertain forecast with substantial downside risk.​

---

#### **Federal Reserve Policy Dilemma and Interest Rate Trajectory**

#### **Competing Mandates Force Rate Cut Despite Inflation Above Target**

The Federal Reserve's October 2025 rate cut to 3.875% (down from 4.33%) represents a pivot from inflation-fighting mode toward employment protection. Policymakers cited "increasing downside risks to employment in recent months" as the primary justification, even as they acknowledged that "inflation has moved up since earlier in the year and remains somewhat elevated."​

US Unemployment Rate vs Federal Funds Rate - 2025

Fed's Dual Mandate Dilemma:

- Inflation Argument for Holding: 3.0% CPI remains 100bp above 2% target; sticky core inflation at 3.1%; tariffs pose upside inflation risks​
- Employment Argument for Cutting: Unemployment rising to 4.3%, highest since October 2021; employee confidence in job-finding declining; weakening labor force participation​
- Market Uncertainty Argument: Rising VIX readings, equity market volatility, and AI bubble concerns suggesting financial stability risks from maintaining restrictive rates​
The Fed's forward guidance for December 2025 is notably cautious—rate cut officials are split, with skeptics warning against complacency and pointing to potential tariff-inflation transmission risks. ING's base case expects a December cut followed by "at least two more rate cuts" in 2026, requiring further dollar weakness for growth recovery.​

#### **Treasury Yield Dynamics Signal Recession Concerns**

The yield curve evolution through 2025 reveals market anxiety about growth prospects. The 2-year yield declined from 4.34% in April to 3.47% in October before rising slightly to 3.62% in November, while the 10-year remained relatively stable around 4.30%. This compression in the yield curve from late April's more normal slope (inverted, with 2-year at 3.86% above 10-year) to a flattened curve signals investor expectations for near-term rate cuts followed by slower long-term growth.​

---

#### **Consumer Spending and Real Estate Market Stalling**

#### **Housing Market Deepens Structural Weakness**

The residential real estate market has decelerated sharply, reflecting both mortgage rate pressures and affordability constraints. Redfin's analysis found that approximately 15% of home purchase contracts fell through in June 2025—the highest rate for that month since records began in 2017.​

Housing Market Deterioration:

- Single-family housing starts dipped 4.6% month-over-month in June and fell 10% year-over-year​
- Completions declined 12-15% year-over-year through June 2025​
- Foreclosure starts increased 7% in first-half 2025 compared to same period in 2024​
- Builders face rising resale inventory and diminishing buyer demand, particularly in Texas and Florida​
The housing slowdown is critical because real estate serves as both a wealth store for middle-income households and an economic activity generator (construction, materials, labor). The deteriorating housing market directly impacts consumer confidence, particularly for those with home equity exposure—explaining part of the K-shaped sentiment divergence.

#### **Corporate Earnings Growth Moderating**

While corporate earnings beat expectations in Q1 2025 (growing 13% year-over-year, exceeding forecasts by nearly 6%), forward expectations have moderated. Wall Street consensus for full-year 2025 earnings growth is 8.8%, down from 13% realized in Q1, with 2026 expectations at 13.5%. However, these consensus estimates assume stable macroeconomic conditions that increasingly appear unlikely given mounting headwinds.​

Goldman Sachs' forward P/E assumption of 21x earnings in 10 years represents a 10% decline from the current 23x multiple, suggesting that even bull-case forecasts embed significant valuation compression.​

---

#### **Geopolitical and Policy Uncertainties**

#### **US-China Trade Tensions and Structural Vulnerabilities**

The recent re-escalation of US-China trade tensions—manifested in Washington's tariff threats and Beijing's rare earth export controls—creates structural fault lines in technology supply chains. The constant escalation around chip access restrictions and export controls represents a "direct attack on the future earnings of these high-flying companies," acting as a structural vulnerability rather than cyclical risk.​

This geopolitical friction explains part of the recent technology weakness, as markets recognize that even strong near-term AI adoption may face headwinds from supply chain fragmentation and export restrictions.​

#### **Dollar Weakness Amid Fed Cuts**

As the Federal Reserve cuts rates ahead of other major central banks, the dollar has weakened. The DXY index hovering around 96.5-97.0 represents softness from earlier 2025 highs, with technical support around 96.5 and resistance near 99.5-100.0. The weakness reflects capital rotation out of dollar assets into gold, euro-area assets, and emerging market bonds as investors reassess relative returns.​

This currency dynamic has mixed implications: dollar weakness supports US exporters but increases import costs (compounding tariff-driven inflation), creates repatriation headwinds for US multinationals, and potentially signals declining reserve currency demand.

---

#### **Investment Implications for Client Advisory**

#### **Portfolio Positioning for a Two-Scenario Economy**

For a client like Connor Barwin managing substantial assets through the Wharton Investment Competition framework, the current macroeconomic backdrop suggests critical portfolio adjustments:

Scenario 1: Soft Landing (Base Case):

- GDP growth moderates to 2.5% as tariff drag offsets Fed stimulus​
- Unemployment stabilizes around 4.3-4.5% with gradual recovery in 2026​
- Inflation gradually declines toward 2% target as tariffs are absorbed and energy prices stabilize​
- Technology sector sustains valuations once AI ROI concerns ease and tariff pass-through clarifies
- S&P 500 reaches 7,600 by end-2026 (Goldman Sachs forecast), representing 11% gain from current levels​
Scenario 2: Hard Landing (Downside Risk):

- Labor market weakness accelerates, pushing unemployment above 5% and entering recession territory
- Inflation remains elevated (2.5-3.0%) due to tariff pass-through, limiting Fed cut capacity
- Technology sector experiences 20-30% correction from peaks as AI ROI becomes a material concern
- Credit spread widening and increased default risk in vulnerable sectors (real estate, consumer discretionary)
- S&P 500 experiences 15-25% pullback from peaks

#### **Strategic Asset Allocation Recommendations**

Given this macro backdrop, a defensively-positioned client portfolio should emphasize:

- Reduced Technology Exposure: Trim mega-cap technology from overweight positions. Current valuations embed strong AI adoption assumptions that market volatility suggests may not materialize. Diversify toward value stocks with stronger balance sheets and lower tariff exposure.
- Inflation Protection: Allocate to real assets (infrastructure, commodities, inflation-protected securities) given sticky inflation and tariff-driven price pressures. The 1.3% near-term price-level impact justifies hedging real purchasing power erosion.
- Sector Rotation: Favor sectors with lower tariff sensitivity (financials, healthcare, utilities) over rate-sensitive and tariff-exposed cyclicals. Avoid real estate exposure given structural housing weakness.
- International Diversification: The US dollar weakness and geopolitical fragmentation suggest balanced international equity exposure provides diversification benefits while capturing non-dollar appreciation.
- Fixed Income Duration: While rates have stabilized near 3.6-4.3%, the yield curve compression suggests near-term rate stability. Ladder maturities across 2-7 year duration to capture yield while maintaining flexibility for potential recession-driven duration extension.

---

#### **Critical Risks to Monitor**

Downside Tail Risks:

- Federal government shutdown extending beyond current resolution, further eroding consumer confidence and investment
- Tariff escalation triggering reciprocal retaliation and supply chain disruption beyond current forecasts
- Technology sector earnings disappointments as AI adoption ROI fails to materialize
- Credit market stress from refinancing burden on vulnerable corporates and households
- Geopolitical escalation (US-China, Middle East, Ukraine) constraining policy flexibility
Upside Opportunities:

- Fed pivot to accommodative stance supporting equity multiples if inflation proves transitory
- Tariff revenue absorption generating fiscal space for stimulus without debt-to-GDP deterioration
- Technology sector normalization providing compelling valuations for long-term growth investors
- Corporate earnings resilience despite macro headwinds, sustained by pricing power and cost discipline

---

#### **Conclusion**

The US economy in late 2025 presents an increasingly difficult macro backdrop for investment strategy. While headline growth remains respectable and equity markets remain elevated, the underlying trajectory points toward mounting headwinds: sticky inflation, deteriorating labor market conditions, collapsing consumer confidence, technology sector vulnerability, and substantial tariff-driven economic drag. The Federal Reserve's rate cutting cycle, though appropriate given labor market weakness, conflicts with inflation remaining above target and creates policy uncertainty.

For the Wharton Investment Competition client advisory context, this macro environment demands strategic humility. Simple "growth at any price" or naive "buy the dip" approaches risk substantial portfolio damage. Instead, client portfolios should be repositioned toward defensive positioning, inflation protection, and reduced technology concentration while maintaining strategic exposure to recovery scenarios. The next 12-18 months will determine whether the economy achieves soft-landing conditions or whether the mounting imbalances produce harder outcomes. Portfolio positioning should reflect both scenarios while emphasizing capital preservation and real return generation in an increasingly uncertain macro environment.
